- Sensor to node: `sensor_id`, generation, its run counter, its `run_id`, the first `seq` it will send (`first_seq`), the newest `seq` it has numbered and synced (`newest_seq`), the count of numbered records it evicted before the node acknowledged them (`drops_channel`), and its clock.
- **This replaces "the oldest `seq` its buffer still holds"** in this rule as merged in #624. `first_seq` carries the same fact, and `newest_seq` with `drops_channel` lets the node match a gap to its declaration (CH-7, CH-15; D3 on #627, note 5). C13 0.2's hello carries more than this (CH-7). This rule requires only these fields.

**SL-14. Every record is ordered by (generation, run counter, `seq`).** The link agent keeps a **run counter** on disk. It starts at 1 for each generation and rises by one at each start of the agent, **of the engine or of the capture stage** (D4 on #624), and so of either of the capture stage's two programs. Each stage ends when its input ends, so a socket holder that restarts alone ends the frame stage's stream too, and the agent sees it (SL-3; D4's B2 on #631). A restarted engine has forgotten the flows that the node still holds open, and the node clears its open-flow table only on a new run. **The agent syncs the counter to disk before it sends the hello,** so a crash after the hello cannot bring the same counter back under another `run_id`, which the node would read as a fork (D5 on #624). `run_id` stays C13's name for a run, and the hello ties it to its counter. The node keeps, for each `sensor_id` and generation, the highest run counter it has accepted, that run's `run_id`, and the last `seq` it committed. It saves them with the commit, so the checks hold across a node restart.

C13 0.2 is stricter on two points, and D3 accepts both. The counter and the `run_id` are synced before the run's first record is numbered (CH-12). And a record is sent only after the sync that covers it (CH-19), so the node can hold more of a run than the sensor's disk holds only when that disk went back.

The node refuses the hello, closes the connection, raises an alarm and writes `sensor.link_refused` when:

| Case | Reason | What it means |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The run counter is below the highest accepted | `run-regressed` | An old run replayed, a restored snapshot, a host re-imaged with no new enrolment, or a copy |
| The run counter equals the highest, with another `run_id` | `run-forked` | Two holders of one key: a copy |
| `sensor_id` or generation differ from the enrolment | `binding` | A stale or wrong enrolment at the sensor |
| On a resumed run, the sensor's newest `seq` is below the `seq` the node took | `run-regressed` | The sensor's disk went back: a restored snapshot, a copy, or a file system that lost synced data (CH-13 row 5a; D3's B2 on #627) |
| The way the sensor's end was pinned differs from the first value the node saw in this generation | `binding` | It changes only with a new enrolment (SL-5; CH-33) |

- **Within a run,** `seq` rises by one from the hello's first `seq`. A `seq` at or below the last one taken in this connection is refused (today's `seq` reason), so a re-ordered stream cannot pass. On a resumed run, a `seq` at or below the last committed one is a resend: it is dropped and counted as a duplicate, and never stored twice.
- **A gap** is counted, as today (`SeqGaps`). It must be matched by drops that the sensor declares in its next heartbeat. A gap the sensor does not declare raises an alarm, because a loss nobody owns is how a record is made to vanish (P5). Declaring a gap needs a counter of the numbered records evicted from the agent's buffer. It was asked of C13 0.2 (D4 on #624), which gives it as `drops_channel`, with the capture spans of what was lost (CH-15, CH-40).
- **A declared loss is still a loss a person must see** (D3 on #627; P5). A declared span switches off the node's own-session check inside it. So the node checks each span, requires a rise in the count for a new or wider one, and opens a coverage gap and an alarm for it. A span that ends after the present is cut once, at the earliest of its own end, the message's clock, and the node's receipt time plus the skew bound. The last term rests on the node's clock alone (CH-40). Without the cut, one span stated to end in the year 2100 would keep the node's one independent check off for good (AP-36 (2)).
- **Records left from the previous run** are flushed before the next run's hello, or declared lost in it. C13 0.2 carries one run on each connection, oldest first, and a new run's hello declares what came before it (CH-16). Either way, no record of an older run follows a newer run's hello.
- *Answers:* AP-34 (a) by replay; a cloned or rolled-back sensor.
- *Test:* SL-T9.

**SL-31. The spans the node holds for a run are kept in the store and transaction that hold the count admitting them, and the node never takes them on the sensor's word after a loss of its own.** This rule is new under P4-4-L6, and its number follows SL-30. It is D3's ruling on the span half of D5's B2 on #634 and D4's B2 on #633, which D1 left to D3 in D4-90 because it decides whether the node trusts the sensor's word.

- **The question.** CH-40 admits a new or wider span only with a rise in the run's `drops_channel`, judged against the D in the cursor. Both documents at `72f09c81` keep the spans in `sensorrun.json` and D in the sensor database. When that file is lost, unreadable or behind its cursor (a rename lost at a power cut, if one can be: an unmeasured assumption, whose code SL-30's rollback bullet gives), an honest resume states spans the node no longer holds, with no rise, because the rise is already in the cursor. The node closes it (`hello`), and does so at every dial while the run holds records not acknowledged (CH-16). Three answers were put:
  - (a) the node takes the stated spans as held, and raises the loss alarm instead of closing (D5's first option);
  - (b) the node keeps the spans in the sensor database, in the transaction that raises D (D4's option);
  - (c) the node holds the link, as for a fold record that cannot be parsed (D5's second option).
- **The ruling: (b).**
  - The spans the node holds for a run, those of `prior.lost` included, each as stated and with its fixed cut end (CH-40), are saved in the sensor database **in the same transaction as the cursor save that accepts them:** the save that raises D, for a new or wider span in `lost`, and CH-13 row 6's save, for `prior.lost`. They are never kept apart from D, so they cannot fall behind the cursor, and a lost rename, if one can happen (SL-30's assumption), cannot touch them. CH-18, CH-40 and the node's design §1.6 change with it, and `sensorrun.json` no longer holds spans.
  - **Which save raises D is CH-40's to settle, and is left to D4** (D5's N8 on #641). A heartbeat carries `drops_channel` and no spans, so it can raise D before the tick that declares the span arrives, and that tick then shows no rise. D5 proposes keeping in the cursor the D at which the run's spans were last accepted, Ds, and judging a span's rise against that (#644 row 7, which D4 owes in CH-40). Whatever D4 writes, SL-31 holds as long as the spans and the D they are judged against are saved in one transaction.
  - **D5 chose the cursor** (D5 on #641): every save that changes the spans is a `SaveSourceCursor` made before the message is acted on, and a new table would change the store's format, which is C5's. The cursor's bound, `MaxLinkCursorBytes`, rises from 192 bytes; D5 proposed 2,048 (#644 row 1), and D1 set it at **4,096** (D1's ruling on #645, comment 6846045180), which #644 states at `2c513d7b`. C13 0.2 on `main` states 192 (CH-18, §13, R-5), and #645 stated 2,048 at `a0fb6550` (CH-18; D6's N9 on #641 at `9d74bb73`). No approved contract changes. Had neither been buildable without a C5 change, D3 would have ruled again; neither (a) nor (c) was a default.
  - **A span's `lost` gap and `sensor-records-lost` are owed from that transaction.** The span is saved with a mark that its gap is owed. **The mark is cleared only once the gap's health event has committed;** until then the marked span is the event's hold, and the gap is never held in `sensorrun.json`, which a crash within its minute can lose, and a lost rename could, if one can happen (D5's N1 on #641; SL-30's assumption). After a restart the node writes the gap of every span still marked, and raises the alarm. A kill between the two writes gives the gap twice, and never not at all. Without the mark, a restart would find the span held and not new, and a gap opened only for a new span would be lost. That was true of `sensorrun.json` too.
  - **Nothing owed is dropped with a position** (D5's N3 on #641). When a saved position reads as none (SL-R6), or row 6's save replaces it, the spans whose gap is still owed and a `prior` whose `sensor.run-loss` has not committed are written first, or carried into the new cursor. *Test:* SL-T24 (e).
  - **With the sensor database unreadable,** the position is unread, and the node does not dial (CH-4, CH-18). **With it lost where a position existed,** which is D4's and D5's to write, the node holds no D and no spans.
    - **Its `node-hello` names the run file's copy of the run, at `seq` 0** (CH-6's form for a run with nothing taken), when the file holds one, and carries no `taken` otherwise. This is D5's rule, which D3 accepts (D5 on #644, comment 6837687519; #644 §1.4 and row 21 of §1.11). It replaces "no `taken`" at `f061c59e`. If the node kept the copy as its saved run and sent no `taken`, the agent would offer that run as a new run with `prior`, and CH-13 row 5 would close it at every dial. If it dropped the copy, an older run could be accepted as new.
    - **D3's condition:** the copy carries its generation, and SL-R6 applies to it. A copy from below the generation at which the position began reads as none. Otherwise, after a `rekey`, the agent's run 1 would be closed as `run-regressed` against the old copy's counter.
    - **The copy may be behind.** With the database gone the file's write number cannot be checked (SL-30). Then the agent does not hold the run that `taken` names, does not apply it (CH-6), and the node judges the agent's true hello against an older counter. **What a stale copy costs:** a copy of the sensor or a restored snapshot whose counter lies between the stale copy's and the true one is accepted, where it should be refused as `run-regressed`. That holds for that window only, and only after two faults on the node. Dropping the copy would cost the same for every earlier run, and holding the link would cost the site's coverage until an administrator acts. A compromised sensor gains nothing new, because it can always move its counter forward (SL-16's residual). Status names the position as taken from the run file with the database lost. *Test:* SL-T24 (f).
    - **Nor can the file's SL-30 counts be checked,** and the cursor's proof is gone. So they read as spent, as SL-30 says of any sensor database absent under a live enrolment, and the dial that sends this hello follows a re-arm. That fails closed.
    - The hello's spans, in `lost` for a resume or `prior.lost` for a new run, are new against a D of 0, admitted only with the count the sensor states, and each opens a `lost` gap with its cut end fixed at its receipt. That is loud, and it trusts nothing that the node still held. A database created shortly before a power cut is one way to reach this case (SL-30's assumption). The lost database also takes the moment the node held as watched, and SL-16 says where the gap at the node's start then begins.
    - **The records the node had taken and lost with its database** are a loss of its own, which no span declares, since the agent trimmed them on the node's acknowledgement. #644 raises `sensor-gap-undeclared` once for them (§1.8, "After a lost sensor database"), and D3 accepts it: it is loud, and the sensor's word plays no part in it.
- **Why (b), weighed.**
  - *What a compromised sensor gains.* **Under (b), nothing:** every span is judged against state the node keeps as durably as D. **Under (a),** after a loss of the node's own, the node holds whatever the sensor states. A span the node never saw rise goes unchecked, and a span the node had already cut is cut again at the new receipt time: one stated to end in 2100 and first cut an hour before the node's restart is cut again at the restart, and for that hour `own-session-unseen` cannot fire on the sessions not yet judged (D6's B2 on #639). The sensor cannot cause the node's loss. But it can state a wider span at every resume at no cost, because a `hello` close writes no audit record (C8-7), and it wins whenever a loss comes. **Under (c),** it gains no trust.
  - *Coverage while it lasts.* **Under (b)** there is nothing to last: the link is not stopped and nothing is degraded. **Under (a)** the link carries on, and each span taken again is a declared loss, with its gap and alarm, that the own-session check does not judge. **Under (c)** the site is unwatched from the loss until an administrator acts. For a lost file that is not "until the record becomes readable": only a `rekey` or a `replace` frees it, each needs the sensor's console, and the sensor's buffer is discarded, declared (D5's N10 on #634). So a station power cut, after which an operator most wants to see the network, would blind the site until someone reaches the sensor's console.
  - (b) also removes the cause, one fact held in two stores of different durability, instead of choosing how to fail when they differ.
- *Answers:* AP-36 (2). A declared span switches off the node's one independent check on the sensor, so the node must never hold fewer spans, or later cut ends, than it accepted.
- *Test:* SL-T24.

**D1's alarm half, checked** (D4-90 asked D3 to check it against SL-30 and the `prior` rule). D1's rule: a node-side loss or rollback of `sensorrun.json` never silently clears a standing alarm, never rolls back or re-arms SL-30's dial count, and never loses or doubles a `prior` or the run's `config_digest` without a status that names it. D1's rule is D4's and D5's to write. The check below is D3's. Where it finds the rule incomplete, the remedy is a rule of this note, stated in its own place and tested there: SL-30's rollback bullet, SL-31's cursor, and the after-loss rules for `sensor-transmitted` and `sensor-egress-absent` in §11.3.

- **It holds for a run file that is lost or unreadable.** **It missed a file that reads well and is behind,** which a lost rename would give, if one can happen (D4's B2 on #633; SL-30's assumption). Nothing in either document told such a file from a current one. The check D4 proposed, the D the spans were saved under against the cursor's, sees a rollback only across a rise in D or a new run. It does not see one across an SL-30 count save, a re-arm's spent hash, an alarm raise or a held event, each of which writes the file without moving the cursor. SL-30's rollback bullet now requires the node to tell, and the node's design meets it with a write number (#644 §1.1), which is not built. "Lost" below means lost, unreadable, behind, or missing where the database shows a file was written. A file missing where none was ever written is a new node's, and is none of these.
- **It also missed a file that cannot be compared** (D6's B4 on #641 at `9d74bb73`). With a sensor's database lost, absent, unread or without a cursor, "behind" is judged against the other databases only, and on a node with one sensor against none, so a stale file reads as current. SL-30 made that sensor's counts spent whatever the file says; nothing did the same for the file's other values. A power cut that loses both the database's creation and the rename of the write that raised `sensor-transmitted` would start the node with the alarm down, and it would not come back if the sensor's next run no longer sends. **So, under a live enrolment, a run file that cannot be compared with that sensor's database reads as lost for every value it holds for that sensor, and for every pair that sensor's enrolments name,** as it does for SL-30's counts. **Two exceptions, each of which only moves something earlier or is checked again:** the copy of the earlier of W and R (D6's N3 on #641 at `da7a84ee`), which serves as a bound that a stale copy only moves earlier (SL-16), and the copy of the run that `node-hello` names, which SL-31 accepts with its stated cost. *Test:* SL-T24 (g), its third case.
- **What a lost file does to each value it held,** as D5 places them (D5 on #641). The per-run values move into the cursor, where a lost file cannot reach them; the rest stay in the file:
  - **In the cursor (SL-31):** the spans and their marks; `prior`, with a mark until its `sensor.run-loss` commits; the run's `config_digest`; and D5's baseline for `sensor-transmitted` and `sensor-egress-absent` (the last `frames_outgoing`, and whether `egress_check` was carried). This is D3's preference, put `prior` and the digest in row 6's transaction, taken by D5.
  - **SL-30's counts:** spent, unless a cursor of the live generation proves the pair. **The spent re-arm hashes:** rebuilt, so a request file put back is refused. **The cap's last record times:** the cap holds from the start (C8-5).
  - **The standing `sensor-transmitted`:** raised until an administrator clears it (§11.3). *Test:* SL-T24 (g).
  - **Held events, held coverage gaps included** (#628 §1.9 item 5, "Held events and a restart"): named in status as possibly lost. That is not enough for a gap, because a gap must never be lost (SL-16). **A gap is never held only in the file:** a `lost` gap's hold is its marked span (SL-31); for every other cause, a lost file opens a gap that reaches back to the earlier of the cursor's W and R (SL-16), which covers any held gap after that moment. No held gap lies before it, because neither moves past an open gap until that gap's closing event has committed (#644 §1.9 item 5), with one exception: at an unwatched close the raise is saved before the event commits, and the gap is held in a cursor field of its own, which the node writes first at its start (the floor, below; D6's N2 on #641 at `cebfff61`). A gap waiting for its budget is held in the cursor, not in the file. With the sensor database lost too, the gap reaches back to the file's copy of that moment, which is never later than the node's W and R when the file was written, nor than a gap then held for the floor, or to the enrolment's time (SL-16). `records-auditor` on #641 found this case uncovered. *Test:* gap leg 14.
  - **The poison record** (#628 §1.9 item 9): a lost record costs at most one more panic at that observation. That is loud (`sensor-failed`), the panic writes the record again, and a skipped observation is counted as refused, so none is skipped silently.
- **`prior`.** In the cursor, a lost or stale file can no longer lose it. A lost sensor database can, and then status names a lost `prior`, and CH-16's sentence that a crash does not lose the declaration says so. **Doubled:** a kill between the `sensor.run-loss` commit and the clearing of its mark gives the event twice, because the health stream is in another database. D1's wording allows that with a status that names it. **The event carries the run's generation, counter and `run_id`,** so that an upstream reader, who sees no status, can tell one declaration sent twice. D5 found that this needs no C4 change. **A second way to double it** (D6's N8 on #641 at `9d74bb73`): with the sensor database lost and a run-file copy that is behind, the node can judge a run whose `sensor.run-loss` it already wrote as new, and write it again. Only the status that says the position came from the run file names it, and the run's identity in the event lets a reader tell it.
- **`config_digest`.** In the cursor, a lost file no longer loses it. With the sensor database lost it is learned again from the next hello, and status names that. It is the sensor's word either way, and row 8 still compares it with the configured digest.

**SL-15. The node acknowledges what it has taken, not what it has received.** This rule as merged in #624 said "committed". C13 0.2 acknowledges what the node has *taken*, and D3 accepts it (CH-17; D3 on #627, note 6). A record is taken once it is committed to the store, saved in the intake hold's checkpoint, a duplicate, a `seq` that was never received and is counted as a gap, or refused or dropped for good. **The count of a refused or dropped record is durable before the acknowledgement passes it** (D1's ruling 2 on #627): the run's refused and dropped totals are saved with the position, so a node crash never erases the only trace of a record that the sensor has trimmed. The acknowledgement names a run and the highest `seq` taken, and never more than the position the node has saved. The link agent trims its buffer only up to it. After a break, a continuing run resumes from the acknowledged `seq` plus one. Records that fell out of the buffer are lost, declared and counted. This is the lesson of C-04 and E-01: an acknowledgement is worth what the receiver had made durable.

- *Why "taken".* A record refused for good must not block the stream behind it, and the hold's checkpoint is durable.
- *Test:* SL-T9.

**SL-16. A re-image and a copy.**

- **A re-image destroys the key** (SL-1), so the host needs a new enrolment at the next generation, and its run counter starts again at 1 under it. The node's own stream numbers carry on, and the detector's checkpoint counts in those, so nothing on the node resets. The open-flow table is cleared, as on any new `run_id` today.
- **A re-image that keeps the key** (a TPM-held key) loses the run counter, so the node refuses it as `run-regressed` until an administrator enrols it again. That is deliberate: a re-image is an event a person attests to.
- **A copy** holds the same key and the same state. The node cannot tell a copy's first connection from the original's restart. It can tell once both have connected: the second arrives with a counter the node has already seen, under another `run_id`. On `run-forked` the node stops dialling that sensor and holds the link until an administrator enrols it again **with a new key**, or revokes it, because one of two key holders is false and the node cannot tell which. Enrolling again with the same key would leave the copy in place, so the node denies it (SL-6; D5 on #624).
- **The hold is durable** (D3's B4 on #627). The node keeps the held state with its sensor state, so a restart of the node does not dial the sensor again (CH-18; #628 §1.3). If that state cannot be written, the dialer stays off for the run, and the next start derives the hold from the fork's own audit record (`sensor.link_refused`, `run-forked`; §11.2, C8-4). The fold holds an enrolment only on a `run-forked` record of that enrolment's own generation, and the cap on that record is keyed by the generation, so that a second fork after a new enrolment always writes one (C8-5; ruling 2 on #631).
- **What a fork costs.** The network at that sensor is unwatched from the hold until someone makes a new key on the sensor host, which is a visit to the site. The coverage gap is recorded and the alarm stands for all of it. The sync of the run counter before the hello (SL-14) is what keeps an honest crash from causing one.
- **Every interval the platform did not watch is a coverage gap, on the node's clock, whatever caused it** (D4-90's gap rule, P4-4-L6). It replaces D1's first text, which said that a link drop and the node's start are not gaps; D6 found that it failed open (B1 on #639). The causes are one closed set, the same in CH-45, the node's design §1.9 item 5, C8-7 and here. **Names are CH-45's to give, and this note gives none.** On `main` CH-45 names two, `blind` and `lost`. The node's design as amended in #644 (§1.9 item 5) proposes eight tokens, which D4 states in CH-45. They match the first eight causes below one for one, and D3 accepts them. The ninth cause, a heartbeat silence on a link that stays up, is new at this revision, and its token is D4's (D6's B1 on #641 at `9d74bb73`):
  - a held link, such as a fork's above, the dial limit or a `renew`'s close until the sensor's end holds it, and every other hold the node makes on a decision of its own other than a revocation, as #644 §1.9 item 5 lists them (the state or position unread, the store not open, the link key, a pending revocation file, an enrolment that the configuration does not match or that has expired, an asset's address, the platform floor): `held`; a sensor revoked: `revoked`; a sensor never back after its link dropped: `link`;
  - the sensor host down: `link` while it lasts, and `restart` once the next run's hello is read, since the node cannot tell it from a network fault by itself; a sensor restart during an outage: `restart`;
  - the node started with no sensor running: `node-start`;
  - C13's own two: records the sensor declares lost (`lost`; CH-40, SL-31), and every interface blind (`blind`; CH-31);
  - a stop of the node's own sensor work, which leaves an interval unwatched whatever the link does: `sensor-failed` (D3's addition, from D3 on #628; D5 took it);
  - **a ninth cause: heartbeats silent on a link that stays up** (D6's B1 on #641 at `9d74bb73`). Ticks are not buffered and keep the link inside `IdleTimeout`, so a wedged agent or buffer can stop the heartbeats with no connection's end, and the next current heartbeat in the same run would move W across the silence. **While no gap is open and the link is current, no current heartbeat for `HeartbeatGapMax` on the node's monotonic clock, counted from the receipt of W's heartbeat, opens a gap** (from the earlier of W and R, below). It is written `open` as a `link` gap is: once it has stood 60 s from W, or 60 s after it opened on the node's monotonic clock where that is earlier (below). It closes as covered only by the chain below, and a stall leaves it unwatched. **Its cause ends at the first current heartbeat whose lateness (CH-23) is under `HeartbeatGapMax`,** not at the first current heartbeat (D6's N1 on #641 at `467c1dd6`): a heartbeat delayed behind a flood by more than 30 s and less than `late_bound_s` is current, and a chain judged at it would hold too few heartbeats for the fourth part, so watched time would close as unwatched. Judged once the heartbeats arrive on time, the chain holds every delayed one, and heartbeats that were only delayed close the gap as covered. **Like a `link` gap, it becomes `restart`** when the node reads, before it closes, the hello of a run that began inside it (D6's N6). **When it and a connection's end fall due at the same evaluation, one gap opens, with cause `link`,** since a gap of either cause opens only while no gap is open; and when it opens first, a connection's end adds `link` to `causes` (D6's N6). Its token is D4's to give in CH-45: CH-45 takes `silent` at #645's `1c454cb6`, and #644 lists it with the other eight at `b4519c63` (D6's N4: at `13494645` it did not). CH-44's `sensor-no-heartbeat` alarm stands as it is: the alarm says the heartbeats stopped, and the gap carries the interval. *Test:* gap leg 16.
  - **Where a gap starts** (D6's B3 on #641 at `9d74bb73`; D6's B2 on #641 at `467c1dd6`; D6's B1 on #641 at `da7a84ee`, which replaces "the earliest of W and every start its causes give"). **A gap never starts at the moment the node notices its cause,** whatever the cause. A sensor host that loses power sends no FIN, so the node sees the loss only at `IdleTimeout`, 30 s (CH-4); a gap opened at the close would leave that 30 s, and up to a heartbeat interval before it, in no gap. It starts at the earlier of W and R:
    - **R, the last rise:** the last current heartbeat in which an interface's `packets` rose **and that interface's `carrier` was not `down`**, placed on the node's clock as W is (below: the earlier of its receipt and its `at_utc` + E). A rise counted with its carrier `down` would not do (D6's cables pulled between two heartbeats). W can run up to `blind_after_s` ahead of R, because W moves on every heartbeat with a rise within `blind_after_s`; so a flat stretch that began before W began at R. **While a gap is open, R does not move, as W does not,** so a gap's start is fixed when it opens. R is saved with W, in the same writes (below). **R is never below the floor (next),** so after a gap closed unwatched R is the later of the last rise and that gap's end.
    - **The floor** (D6's B1 on #641 at `8f78d057`, D6's fix, which D1 accepted). **When a gap other than `lost` closes unwatched (`covered: false`), W and R become its `to_utc` wherever they are earlier or have no value, and the floor becomes that `to_utc`, whatever it was before. After that, neither takes a value below that `to_utc`,** B2's move back included (below). Without it, R stayed where it stood before the gap: a `restart` or never-watched gap can close at a run's first heartbeat, which is never a rise; a `silent` gap's late heartbeats move nothing while it is open; and nothing said that a `blind` gap's closing rise moved R. A later gap then started at the earlier gap's start, and if it closed as covered it claimed an interval already written unwatched, with the same `from_utc`, which is how a reader pairs records (the event carries no gap identifier). Every interval from the earlier gap's start to its `to_utc` lies in that gap, so the floor leaves nothing in no gap. **It applies only at an unwatched close.** Raised at a covered close, it would start a later `blind` gap at the covered gap's end where the flat stretch began inside it, which is D6's B1 at `da7a84ee` again. **So no gap other than `lost`, and other than one that starts at a bound, starts before the `to_utc` of an earlier gap closed unwatched, where no step back of the node's wall clock lies between the two gaps** (D6's B1 on #641 at `cebfff61`). Across a step back the two times are on different clocks, and the step rule below places the later gap: leg 9's fifth case expects a `node-start` gap that starts before the `silent` gap's written end. That is the assertion of the legs below. D3 adds four things the fix needs:
      - **A step back of the wall clock moves the floor back with W, R and the copy** (below). Left on the old clock, it would hold W and R up to the step after the last watched moment on the new clock, which is the late start that the step rule exists to prevent.
      - **W, R and the floor are never later than the node's wall clock** (D6's B1 on #641 at `cebfff61`; D6's fix, which D1 accepted, with W and R added by D3). Each is a receipt of the node's or earlier, so none is later than the wall clock unless the clock stepped back after it was set. **One rule keeps them so, the step rule** (D1's direction on D6's B1 on #641 at `02773aa1`). It replaces the list of check points at `02773aa1`: the tick, each placement of W or R, and each gap's open.
        - **The rule.** **Before the node takes a time from its wall clock for a gap, for W, R or the floor, or for the run file's copy, it runs the step check.** A time for a gap is its open, its close, its `from_utc` or `to_utc`, the floor's raise, a `lost` gap's cut end, and the time a never-watched gap starts from. A time for W or R is a heartbeat's receipt, and the ticks' receipts that E is taken from. The node also runs the check at its tick.
        - **What the check does.** It compares the wall clock with the monotonic clock since the check before. **It acts only on a step back of more than `max_skew_ms` (2,000 ms) between two checks** (D6's N3 on #641 at `6723bf90`): a smaller difference moves nothing and marks nothing, so a time service that slews the clock does not mark every gap open through the slew. **A step back found moves W, R, the floor, the copy and E's held samples back by the step.** **The floor in this rule is the gap floor above;** the capture floor, M and the start bound (SL-17) are readings of the sensor's clock, and this rule never moves them back or caps them (D6's N4 on #641 at `94834c5a`). The node saves the cursor and the run file at once, writes `time.node-stepped` once for that step, and marks `clock_stepped` on any gap open when the step is found, the gap being opened or closed included, **and on any gap closed and not yet written: the gap held for the floor, and a gap waiting for its budget or the ceiling** (D6's N1 on #641 at `6723bf90`). A later gap merged into one of those would otherwise carry times on two clocks with no mark. Then any of W, R and the floor still later than the wall clock is a step back the check could not measure: it takes the wall-clock time, and is marked and written in the same way. Only then is the time taken. A gap's written `from_utc` is never moved: the mark names the step. **A gap marked `clock_stepped` is never closed as covered by the chain:** it closes unwatched (`covered: false`), at the first current heartbeat after its cause ends that shows capture (D1's ruling on #641 at `bb7f0d91`). Its length on the wall clock is not the length it stood, and steps are rare, so the simplest rule that fails closed is to claim none of it as watched. *Test:* leg 9's third case.
        - **Why before, and why every such read** (D6's B1 on #641 at `02773aa1`). A time taken before the check is on whichever clock the node last checked. At an unwatched close the raise is not a placement, and while a gap is open nothing places W or R, so the last check before a close can be a tick 60 s earlier. Take a `silent` gap from R0 with the clock stepped back 60 s, 5 s before its close at t1. Unchecked, the record goes out with no `clock_stepped`, though the step lies inside the gap, and the floor is raised to t1 on the new clock. The next placement then finds the step and moves the floor back to t1 − 60. The next heartbeat's `at_utc` + E, about t1 − 15, is above that, so W and R take it, and a `link` gap opened one heartbeat later starts 15 s inside the gap already written unwatched. Checked first, the close finds the step, marks the gap and raises the floor on the new clock, and no later move takes it below. The rounds at `cebfff61` and `02773aa1` each found one more read like this: a start, a placement, an open, then a close. So the rule names the kinds of time, not the places where they are read.
        - **At a start** there is no earlier check to compare with: only wall-clock times survive a reboot, and across a restart of the node's process alone the node keeps no other (D6's N4 on #641 at `6723bf90`; "Across a restart", below). So the check cannot measure a step made during the stop, and can only cap: W, R and the floor saved before a step back during the stop take the wall-clock time. The `node-start` gap's `from_utc` is the saved moment, which is not a reading of the clock, so the gap still reaches back to it and names the step (below). Say W and R are saved at 12:00:00 and the clock is stepped back five minutes during a one-minute stop. Without the cap, a `link` gap opened at 11:57:00 starts at 12:00:00, so 11:57:00 to 12:00:00 is in no gap and nothing marks the start late. **This was the start rule at `02773aa1`, and it is no longer a rule of its own:** a start is the one place where the check cannot measure, and the cap is all it can do there (D6's N2 on #641 at `02773aa1`).
        - **The cap waits for the `node-start` gap's close** (found in D3's sweep; the start rule at `cebfff61` and `02773aa1` had the same fault). The cap moves W, R and the floor past the stop, since the wall-clock time at the start lies a stop's length after the saved moment on the new clock, and the stop is what the `node-start` gap holds. A cap saved while that gap is open, with the node killed before the gap is in the health stream, would leave the next start to reach back only to the first start's wall-clock time, with the stop in no gap and nothing marked. That is a move of W past an open gap, which the rule below forbids. While the `node-start` gap is open, nothing places W or R and no other gap opens, so the saved values move no gap's start. **They would delay the gap's `open` record,** which counts 60 s from W, and the next bullet bounds that (D6's B1 on #641 at `6723bf90`, which corrects "do no harm" here). So the check at its close caps them, before its `to_utc` is taken. A check at a tick before then only measures. A step it finds moves the values back by the step, which never takes them past the stop. **The comparison at the start marks the `node-start` gap `clock_stepped` at once** where W, R or the floor is later than the wall clock (D6's optional fix to B1 at `6723bf90`). The mark moves nothing, so the gap's `open` record, whose `from_utc` is then later than its own writing, names the step. Only the cap waits. *Test:* leg 9's tenth case, and leg 10's fourth for the mark.
        - **The `open` record is never late by a step** (D6's B1 on #641 at `6723bf90`). A `link`, `node-start` or `silent` gap is written `open` once it has stood 60 s from W, and the never-watched gap once it has stood 60 s from `enrolled_at`. Both are wall-clock times that no step moves in two cases: across a restart, where the saved W stays uncapped until the `node-start` gap's close, and `enrolled_at`, which is a written `from_utc`. Counted on the wall clock alone, a five-minute step back during a one-minute stop writes the `node-start` gap `open` about 300 s after the start, while `sensor-unreachable` is due at 60 s and writes no health event, so for four minutes the stream holds nothing for a sensor nobody watches. Only the step bounds that, and a clock run ahead while the node is up and corrected at its next boot can leave W ahead by hours. **So a gap is written `open` at the earlier of two moments: when it has stood 60 s from W (or from `enrolled_at`) on the wall clock, and 60 s after the gap opened on the monotonic clock.** With no step the first is never the later, since a gap opens at its `from_utc` or after it, so nothing changes. A step forward makes the first earlier, which only writes the record sooner. *Test:* leg 10's fourth case.
        - **Why the cap reaches W and R** (D6's N2 on #641 at `02773aa1`, which corrects this note's reason at `02773aa1`). A gap starts at the earlier of W and R. The floor's raise lifts each of them only where it is earlier, so after a step the node has not measured, an uncapped pair stays on the old clock until W's next placement or R's next rise, and a gap opened before then starts late. Capping R alone would be enough for both the start and the copy, since each is the earlier of W and R, and R has a value whenever W has one: W is set only while no gap is open, and a sensor's first watched moment always follows a gap that closed unwatched, which gives R a value (below). W is capped as well only because the check treats every value it moves alike: one rule, and the cap can only move W earlier. So leg 9's seventh case tests the cap on the pair, and its control caps neither of them.
        - **It fails closed.** Without a step back, none of W, R and the floor is later than the wall clock, so the cap never fires on a clock that did not step back. A step back found only moves values earlier and adds a mark. What lies between the saved moment and a start is in the `node-start` gap, which names the step. A step forward is not looked for: it only lengthens a gap.
        - **What the threshold leaves** (D6's N3 on #641 at `6723bf90`). Each check lets a step back of up to 2,000 ms pass, so a value can be late on the new clock by up to that much for each check made since it was set. It is never later than the wall clock, since the check caps any value that is, and so never late by more than its own age. A slew never leaves a value later than the wall clock, because a slewed clock still runs forward. W moves at each heartbeat, so a gap's start at W is late by at most about 2,000 ms under a slew. Only a source that steps the clock back by just under 2 s at check after check makes it more, up to the age of W or R at the gap's open. **R's age at a gap's open can reach `blind_after_s`,** 300 s by default and up to 3,600 s (CH-31), where an accumulated step shows (D6's N3 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). **Steps that add up raise `sensor-clock-skew` only where the sensor's clock is independent of the node's:** a source that serves both hosts moves both, and the alarm, which compares the two, sees nothing. The threshold opens no route in the gap rule; in the tie it would, which is why SL-17's rule on the two clocks compares end to end and not by steps found.
        - **The reads it covers,** found by sweeping this note: W's and R's placement at a heartbeat's receipt, and the ticks' receipts in E (W, below); every gap's open; every gap's close and its `to_utc` (a chain's close, the unwatched close of a failed chain, of `blind`, of `revoked` and of a never-watched gap) and the floor's raise to it; a `lost` gap's cut end (SL-31); the time a never-watched gap starts from, when the node writes its `sensor.enrolled` record while running; the run file's copy and the gap held for the floor, which are taken from those times; and the comparison at a start (above). **Outside it,** since none is a gap's time, W, R, the floor or the copy: the enrolment's `not_after` against the wall clock (SL-8); the cap's last record times, compared at a start (C8-5); and the received time on each record, and the skew alarm and the tie (SL-17, which has its own rule on the two clocks). The sweep at `bb7f0d91` classed the 60 s before `open` as monotonic, and it was not (next).
        - **The durations that are wall-clock differences** (D3's second sweep, for D6's B1 on #641 at `6723bf90`: every length the note computes, not only the times it takes):
          1. **The 60 s before `open`,** counted from W or from `enrolled_at`. Now bounded by 60 s on the monotonic clock from the gap's open (above).
          2. **The chain's fourth part across a restart,** from the saved W to the close. Its residual is stated under "Across a restart" (below).
          3. **CH-23's lateness and E's samples,** each a receipt of the node's less a time of the sensor's. The check runs before each receipt is taken and moves E's held samples, so both sides are on the new clock. A step forward overstates lateness until E's samples age out, which fails closed: a heartbeat read as later moves nothing sooner. Under the threshold, either can be off by up to 2,000 ms.
          4. **C8-5's hour across a restart,** from `sensorrun.json`'s last record time to the time after the start (#644 §1.9 item 6; within a run it is monotonic). A step back reads the later saved time as now (row 23), so the hour starts again: fewer records, with the refusals counted and carried in the next `count`. A step forward ends the hour early, at most one record more for each start, which the stretch already allows. No change.
          5. **The tie** (SL-17). `t_sensor + E` is compared with times the node stored on its wall clock, and nothing stored is re-timed (#644 §1.5). **This one failed open, on the live link as well as after a break.** The sweep at `c0cf1ecc` found it only for a step left inside a short break; D6 found it on the live link, across a step that one time source makes on both clocks or a correction of the node's clock alone (D6's B1 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`), and D5 after a break, across a step found while a backlog is read (D5 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). CH-22 sees none of these, because it compares the two clocks and they agree after the step. SL-17 gains one rule on the two clocks, below.
          6. **Comparisons with the clock, not durations:** the enrolment's `not_after` (SL-8), where a step back keeps an enrolment past `not_after` by the length of the step: the ordinary limit of a validity date, bounded by the step and named by `time.node-stepped` where the check finds it (D6's N4 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`); a request file's own time against the time the spent set was last rebuilt (SL-30, D5's design with its addition), where a step back refuses a new file until the clock passes the rebuild, which fails closed; and a `lost` gap's cut end, which the step rule covers.
          7. **Everything else is a timer on the monotonic clock:** `HeartbeatGapMax`, `IdleTimeout`, the tick, the cursor's save at most a minute apart, `sensor-unreachable` (#644 Part 2 §1: from the later of the last accepted heartbeat and the start), `blind_after_s` at a single heartbeat, the gap budget's hour (4 an hour for each budget, #644 §1.9 item 5: #644 §1.4 keeps no budget count in the cursor, so the hour is a window in memory on the monotonic clock, which a start begins again; D6's N4 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`), and a gap's length within a run. That length is read as the monotonic time since the receipt of W's heartbeat, plus W's distance below that receipt, taken at the placement. The chain's parts 2 and 3 count by `at_utc`, the sensor's clock in one run, which no step of the node's moves.
        - *Tests:* leg 9's third case (a stepped gap never closed as covered), its sixth (the floor after a step during a stop), its seventh (W and R), its eighth (a step between two ticks, found at a gap's open), its ninth (E's samples), its tenth (the cap waits for the `node-start` gap's close) and its eleventh (the threshold); leg 10's fourth case (the `open` record after a step during a stop); leg 14's fifth case (a step found while a gap is held) and its seventh (a step found while a later gap is open); and leg 16's eighth case (a step found at a gap's close).
      - **The floor adds no field.** It is held in memory, and W and R are saved at or above it. **A new W or R below the floor is taken at the floor** (D6's N2 on #641 at `02773aa1`: the controls of leg 9's fifth case and leg 16's seventh assumed it, and a W left where it was would hide the floor's cap). **At a node start the floor is where the `node-start` gap begins** (the earlier of the saved W and R, or the bound), **and the check at that gap's close caps it** (above). Nothing before it is in no gap, and a sensor that states early `at_utc`s after the start still cannot take W below an earlier unwatched gap's end.
      - **A crash between the close and the save of the raise** must leave neither the gap lost nor W and R below its end. The closing event is in the health stream, another database, so it cannot share a transaction with the cursor. A node that commits the event and saves the raise with the next save, killed between them, starts the `node-start` gap at the earlier gap's start. **The mechanism is D5's, "the gap held for the floor"** (#644 §1.4 and §1.9 item 5 at `e1bcd304`; D5's comment 6841864173 on #641), which this note adopts. It replaces the way this note offered at `cebfff61`, which could not be built: the cursor holds a gap waiting for its budget only through W, which does not move past it, so a save that raises W past the gap's start cannot hold it there too (D6's N2 on #641 at `cebfff61`). What the mechanism must meet:
        - **The raise is saved first,** with the closed gap held in a cursor field of its own (its `from_utc`, `to_utc`, the cause at its open, its causes, the number of gaps it carries, and one mark for `from_bound` and `clock_stepped`), and the event commits after it. The first cursor save after the commit clears the field.
        - **At a start, a gap found in that field is written first,** `closed` with `covered: false` and its own times and marks. A kill writes the gap twice at worst, and never not at all, and W and R are never saved below its end.
        - **One gap is held there at a time, from the raise until its event commits; a later gap is merged into it** (D6's N4 on #641 at `02773aa1`; D1's preferred fix). A gap other than `lost` that closes unwatched while the held gap's event has not committed is merged into the held gap, as gaps past a budget are merged (#644 §1.9 item 5): its causes are added, the held gap's `to_utc` becomes its `to_utc`, the number of gaps rises by one, and **the merged gap carries `clock_stepped` if either part carries it** (D6's N1 on #641 at `6723bf90`; a step found while the field is held marks the held gap, under the step rule). `from_bound` stays the held gap's. The raise to that end is saved with the merged field, and one event is written for the whole. Once the held gap's event has committed, the next gap closed unwatched takes the field. **Why:** at `02773aa1` the later gap waited in W with its raise, and a gap opened meanwhile started at the earlier of W and R, between the later gap's start and its end, so inside a gap already closed unwatched, and with the same `from_utc` where R had not risen since. Merged, the floor is at the later gap's end before anything else opens, so the invariant above holds with no step. **What it costs:** the watched interval between the two gaps is written inside the merged gap, which fails closed. The later gap is never written `open` before the merge, since no record of a later gap is written while the held gap's event waits (next bullet). The count adds 21 bytes to the field (below).
        - **The order of a sensor's gap records, and how a reader pairs them** (D5's third point on #641 at `6723bf90`; D6's B3 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`, D6's second fix, which replaces the pairing by interval at `c0cf1ecc`; for this merge and for a merge under a budget alike). The event carries no gap identifier, and a reader pairs records by `from_utc`. **While the closing record of a gap other than `lost` waits, held for the floor or waiting for its budget or the ceiling, the node writes no record of a later gap of the same sensor other than `lost`** (D6's B2 on #641 at `50a67730`, D6's wording: at `50a67730` a waiting `lost` record held every later gap behind the `lost` budget, up to an hour, and the rule did not say "of the same sensor", though budgets are per sensor). A `lost` gap keeps its own budget and is never merged with another cause (below), so it is never held behind one, and holds none behind it. So every gap merged into a waiting record was never written `open`, and **a `closed` record pairs with every `open` record of the same sensor, written before it and not yet paired, whose `from_utc` equals its own.** Nothing pairs by interval.
          - **Why every such record** (D6's N4 on #641 at `50a67730`). While the node runs, the order and one open gap at a time leave at most one unpaired `open` record of a given `from_utc`. Across a node stop there can be two: a gap written `open` and still open at the stop, and the `node-start` gap, which reaches back to the same earlier of W and R, since neither moved while the first was open, and is written `open` too. Both stand for the interval from that `from_utc`, and the `node-start` gap's close ends the whole of it, so its `closed` record pairs both. Pairing "the one" left the other reading as open for good, which failed closed but never cleared.
          - **Why this of D6's two fixes.** Pairing by interval, at `c0cf1ecc`, paired a still-open gap away across a step: with the field held, a step back larger than the time since the merged gap's end and shorter than the time since its start puts a later `link` gap's `from_utc` inside the merged record's interval, and a reader read that gap as closed while it stood, for as long as it stood. D6's first fix, pairing a record marked `clock_stepped` by an equal `from_utc` only, keeps the interval rule for every other merge and, once a step marks a merge, leaves the later gap's `open` record, written before the merge, unpaired for good: a gap that closed reads as still open. The second fix removes the interval rule altogether. The order costs nothing a node would otherwise write: a closing record of a gap other than `lost` waits only while it cannot be written, past the ceiling, past its budget or on a store fault, and the same sensor's later gap other than `lost` would meet the same ceiling, budget or fault, since the causes other than `lost` share one budget for each sensor. The 60 s by which a gap is written `open` (the step rule, above) yields to this order as it yields to the ceiling and the budget.
          - **It fails closed.** An `open` record that no record pairs reads as a gap still open, and an equal `from_utc` is the only pairing, so nothing reads as closed that its own record did not close.
          - *Test:* leg 14's sixth case, for this merge; leg 6's second case, for a merge under a budget; leg 6's third case, for a waiting `lost` record and another sensor's waiting record; and leg 14's eighth case, for two `open` records of one `from_utc` across a node stop.
        - **The run file's copy is the earliest of W, R and the held gap's `from_utc`, as the node holds them when it writes the file, and the gap is held from the raise itself,** before the raise is saved (D3, added to D5's mechanism; D6's N1 on #641 at `02773aa1`). The field is in the cursor, so a lost sensor database loses it with the cursor. A copy of the earlier of the raised W and R alone, written to the run file after the raise and before the event's commit, would then start the `node-start` gap at the held gap's end, with the whole held gap in no gap. At `02773aa1` the copy was held back only from the raise's save, and #644 writes the cursor after each write of the run file, so a file written between the raise and its save still took the raised values (D6's N1). **Why this form and not a copy taken from the cursor as saved:** that form also needs the cursor saved before the run file at a step back, since a copy taken from a cursor not yet saved would be on the old clock, which is a second rule. Taken from what the node holds, the copy follows a step at once.
        - **While a gap is held there, the copy never rises** (D6's N2 on #641 at `6723bf90`): each write of the run file takes the earlier of the new value and the copy it replaces. A step back moves W, R and the copy back, but not the held gap's `from_utc`, which is a time to be written. After a step longer than the time since that `from_utc`, W or R is then the earliest of the three, and on a common clock it is later than the held gap's start, so with the node killed before the commit and the sensor database lost, part of the held gap would be in no gap. Of D6's two fixes D3 takes this one: it only ever moves the copy earlier, and moving the held `from_utc` back would be a second exception to "a gap's written `from_utc` is never moved". Once the field is cleared, the copy is again the earlier of W and R. *Test:* leg 14's third case, its stepped half.
        - **The rule it qualifies.** W does not move past an open gap until that gap's closing event has committed or the gap has closed as covered, **except at an unwatched close, whose raise is saved with the gap held in that field before the event commits.** Wherever this note, #644 or #645 states the rule, it carries that exception.
        - **What it costs:** 71 bytes of the cursor (65, and 6 separators: 50 at `02773aa1`, and 21 for the count of merged gaps, a decimal of at most 20 digits as the cursor's other decimals are). The cursor's text form was then at most 2,005 bytes, 2,011 with `unclean` in full, and 909 with no span or `prior` held, within a `MaxLinkCursorBytes` of 2,048 (#644 §1.4 at `d04e2b8b`, which itemises 1,984, 1,990 and 888 without the count). With M (SL-17) it is at most 2,026 bytes, 2,032 with `unclean` in full, and 930 with no span or `prior` held, within the 4,096 of D1's ruling on #645 (comment 6846045180). The floor itself still adds no field.
        - *Test:* leg 14's second case, with D5's second control; its third case for the copy, at two write points and across a step; its fourth for the merge; its fifth and seventh for the mark through a merge; its sixth for the order of the records and the pairing; and its eighth for two `open` records of one `from_utc` across a node stop.
    - **It also closes the case with no R** (D6's B1 on #641 at `8f78d057`). On a sensor's first run, or after a lost database took R with it, a run flat from its first heartbeat had its `blind` gap start at W, up to `blind_after_s` after the flat stretch began, with that stretch in no gap. The never-watched, `node-start` or `restart` gap before it closes unwatched at that run's first heartbeat, so the floor gives R a value there, and the `blind` gap starts no later than that gap's end. Once a gap has closed unwatched, R always has a value.
    - **Every gap the node opens, while it runs or at its start, starts at the earlier of W and R** (D6's B1 on #641 at `da7a84ee`, in the simpler of its two forms, which D1 preferred). **A `lost` gap is the one exception** (D6's N2 on #641 at `da7a84ee`): it is its span (below), which can begin after W, as a span evicted during a cut and declared at the resume does, and it is written with its span's times. A gap opened while the node holds no W starts at the bound below. So a `blind` gap starts at R, and so does any gap whose cause the node notices during a flat stretch.
    - **Why one rule for every gap, from its open.** At `da7a84ee` the start moved back to R in two cases only: when `blind` joined an open gap, and when a chain failed part 2. **A `restart` gap had neither.** No chain runs across a new run, and the old run's heartbeats arrive as a backlog, which is not current, so `blind` never joins. A stretch flat from four minutes before W, cut at W, with a new run three minutes into the cut, was then written from W, while the same stretch with no new run closed from R: the record of a flat stretch depended on whether a run began inside the cut. D6 offered two fixes: judge the old run's heartbeats after W's per heartbeat wherever no chain is judged, or start every gap that closes unwatched at the earlier of W and R. D3 takes the second, as D1 asked, because it covers every route at once, and applies it from the gap's open and not only at its close: **a gap that never closes is in the stream only as `open`,** and written from W it would leave the stretch before W in no record (gap leg 10's third case). Taken at the open, the start needs no move: `blind` joining, a failed chain and a restart all find it at R already, and no closing record carries a start earlier than its open record's.
    - **What it costs.** A gap that follows a flat stretch shorter than `blind_after_s` starts at R, where live no gap would have begun before W: up to that stretch of watched time is written inside a gap, which fails closed. **A gap closed as covered is unchanged in substance:** the chain's part 2 fails wherever a flat stretch reaches `blind_after_s` at a heartbeat of the chain, and no gap closed unwatched lies between R and W, since the floor raises both to its end, so [R, W] inside a covered gap was watched (D6's B1 on #641 at `8f78d057`: without the floor, that held only where no gap lay between them); and **the chain's fourth part counts the gap's length from W, never from R** (below), so the earlier start asks the chain for no more heartbeats than before. The 60 s after which a `link`, `node-start` or `silent` gap is written `open` are still counted from W, and never run past 60 s after the gap's open on the monotonic clock (the step rule, above).
    - *Tests:* gap leg 10's second case and legs 11 to 13, each with a cause that falls 20 s after W, before the ninth cause would (D6's B3 at `467c1dd6`); legs 16 and 17 for R; leg 17's eighth case, a new run inside the cut, and leg 10's third case, a gap that never closes (D6's B1 at `da7a84ee`); for the floor (D6's B1 at `8f78d057`), leg 16's sixth case (R carried past a `silent` gap's close), its seventh (the move back below the floor), leg 15's second case (no R), leg 14's second case (a crash between the close and the save) and leg 9's fifth case (the floor and a step back); and, at `cebfff61` (D6's B1 and N2), leg 14's third case (the copy while a gap is held for the floor) and leg 9's sixth to eighth cases (a step the node does not see); and, at `02773aa1` (D6's B1, N1 and N4), leg 16's eighth case (a step found at a gap's close), leg 9's ninth and tenth cases (E's samples, and the cap at a start), leg 14's third case's second write point and its fourth case (a merge into the held gap); and, at `6723bf90` (D6's B1 and N1 to N3, D5's third point), leg 10's fourth case (the `open` record after a step during a stop), leg 9's eleventh (the threshold), leg 14's third case across a step, its fifth (the mark through a merge) and sixth (the pairing), and leg 6's second case (a merge under a budget); and, at `c0cf1ecc` (D6's B3, N1 and N2), leg 14's sixth case as replaced (the order of the records), its seventh (a step while the later gap is open), and leg 9's eleventh case as reworked (the threshold at any interval); and, at `50a67730` (D6's B2 and N4), leg 6's third case (a waiting `lost` record, and another sensor's) and leg 14's eighth (two `open` records of one `from_utc`).
  - **The gap at the node's start reaches back** to the earlier of W and R, the last moment the node holds durably as watched and the last rise saved with it, so a station power loss that stops both machines leaves no interval in no gap.
  - **An interval is closed as covered** once the backlog that covers it is through (CH-39) and the sensor's heartbeats show capture for it. Observations arriving are not the test, so a quiet interval that was watched is closed. The gap stays open for whatever is not covered.
  - **What "shows capture" means** (D6's B2 on #641 at `9d74bb73`). It is CH-31's blind rule, **flat counts included,** and never the carrier alone. #644 and CH-39 at `a0fb6550` wrote "not every interface's `carrier` `down`", under which a ten-minute cut with every interface's `packets` flat and its carrier `up` closes as covered while CH-44 records the same stretch as `sensor.past-blind`: the gap stream fails open.
    - **A single current heartbeat shows capture** when not every interface is blind under CH-31 at its receipt: some interface has a `carrier` that is not `down` and a `packets` that has risen within the last `blind_after_s` of the node's monotonic time, **or whose run began within it,** since CH-31 starts its comparison again at a run's first heartbeat (D6's B1 on #641 at `8f78d057` reads it so, and so does the chain below). W moves only on such a heartbeat. When every interface is blind, the `blind` gap opens from the earlier of W and R, as above.
    - **A chain shows capture when each of its heartbeats does, under CH-31 applied by `at_utc`** (D6's B1 on #641 at `467c1dd6`, which replaces this note's stretch wording at that head): in each heartbeat of the chain, some interface's `carrier` is not `down` **in that heartbeat**, and that interface's `packets` rose within the `blind_after_s` before it, by `at_utc`. A rise is a `packets` above the same interface's in the run's heartbeat before. The test is per heartbeat, so a carrier `down` on every interface for less than `blind_after_s` fails it, as it opens a `blind` gap live. It is never read as CH-31's windowed state over a stretch, which would need twice `blind_after_s` flat. Every time it compares is the sensor's `at_utc` in one run, so the chain and the rises it looks back to are on one clock.
    - **What the chain looks back to.** With W in the cursor, the node keeps, from W's heartbeat, each interface's `packets` and the `at_utc` of its last rise in the run; for an interface with no rise in the run, the `at_utc` of the run's first heartbeat, since it has been flat from there (CH-31); and R. These are saved with W, in the same writes, so they are never of another moment. **Where the node holds neither a rise nor the run's first heartbeat for an interface, the chain fails.** It never counts from the chain's own first heartbeat: after a node-only restart, a stretch flat from four minutes before W would then look two minutes long and close as covered, where live it is a `blind` gap from R. **The node need not keep the interfaces' names** (D5's note 1 on #641 at `da7a84ee`): #644 holds the values in the bytewise order of the names, since CH-31 holds the run's set of interfaces fixed and the chain never leaves the run. Where the values held do not match the interfaces of the run's coverage statement one for one, the chain fails, as where none is held.
    - **A chain that fails leaves the whole gap open, unwatched,** from its start, which is already the earlier of W and R (above). So a chain that fails on a stretch flat from before W agrees with `sensor.past-blind` before W as well as after, and no start moves.
    - **A gap closes unwatched at the first current heartbeat after its cause ends that shows capture** in this sense. A sensor whose counts are still flat at the reconnect keeps the gap open until an interface's `packets` rise.
    - *Test:* gap leg 17.
  - **A `lost` gap has a budget of its own,** so that link drops cannot delay it, and it is never merged away without its cause, whatever the budget of gaps an hour.
- **D3's conditions on the two times that bound a gap,** since each rests on a clock and the second on the sensor's word:
  - **W, defined once here** (D6's N3 on #641 at `467c1dd6`; CH-45's and #644's symbol): the moment the node holds as watched for a sensor, through which every interval was either watched or written in a committed gap event. While no gap is open it moves, at each current heartbeat that shows capture, to **the earlier of the node's receipt of that heartbeat and the heartbeat's `at_utc` + E** (CH-22's E as it stands at the receipt, its samples moved back by any step back found since each was taken, under the step rule above; D6's B2 on #641 at `da7a84ee`), never below the floor (a value below it is taken at the floor) and never later than the node's wall clock (above), and it does not move past an open gap, but for the raise at an unwatched close (above). R is placed the same way. W is kept in the cursor, with what the chain looks back to (above).
  - **The moment the node holds as watched is never later than one it saw itself:** its own receipt of a heartbeat that showed capture, read while the link was current, and committed. A time the sensor states is never W on its own, **the heartbeat's own `at_utc` included, however close it is to the receipt,** and nor is the commit time of a backlog record. A sensor clock five minutes ahead makes no heartbeat late, so a node that kept `at_utc` as W would leave the first five minutes of a joint stop in no gap (D6's B3 on #641 at `9d74bb73`). **`at_utc` + E serves only to take W, or R, below the receipt.** With the sensor clock five minutes ahead, E cancels the offset, and `at_utc` + E never moves W after the receipt. *Test:* gap leg 1's second case. D5 keeps it in the cursor, saved at most a minute apart, so the gap after a start can begin up to a minute early, which errs toward a longer gap.
  - **Why `at_utc` + E bounds the receipt** (D6's B2 on #641 at `da7a84ee`). A heartbeat stays current up to `late_bound_s` late (CH-23: 180 s by default, up to 3,600 s). A node whose intake is slower than the sensor's record rate, through F-016's stalls or a burst of observations, lets the agent's buffer grow. Heartbeats queue in that buffer and ticks do not, so E takes in only the queue in the stream, and the buffer's wait shows as lateness. If the delay grows by less than 20 s a heartbeat, 30 s never pass without a current heartbeat, and the ninth cause never falls due. Placed at their receipts, R and W would then lie up to that delay after the moment their heartbeat shows. Two cases, with heartbeats about 120 s late:
    - every carrier goes `down` 3 s after a heartbeat made at h: the `blind` gap would open from about h + 120 s, and [h + 3 s, h + 120 s] would be in no gap;
    - the agent stops making heartbeats at X and keeps sending what it holds: W would move to the last receipt, about X + 120 s, the ninth cause would open from there, and [X, X + 120 s] would be in no gap.

    Bounded by `at_utc` + E, each is placed at the moment its heartbeat shows, on the node's clock. Of D6's two fixes D3 takes this one, which D1 preferred. The other, moving neither on a heartbeat 30 s late or more, would leave up to 30 s.
  - **The residual** is the delay that E itself takes in: the least, over the connection's last six ticks, of the network delay and the queue in the stream (CH-22). The bound does not remove it. With the sensor's clock true, a residual above `max_skew_ms` raises `sensor-clock-skew`; a sensor clock ahead can hide up to its offset of it, which is the sensor's word (next).
  - **What a sensor that lies gains: nothing new.** `at_utc` and the ticks' times are both its word, so it sets `at_utc` + E. The bound only ever takes W and R below the receipt, never above it, so whatever it states, W and R are never later than under the receipt rule. A lie can move them earlier, which starts a gap earlier, lengthens the gap for the chain's fourth part, and hides none. Or it can leave them at the receipt by stating a late `at_utc`, which gives back the delay of the case above and no more. A sensor that wants an interval to read as watched can already state heartbeats that show capture (AP-36). **So D3 confirms D1's statement with one qualification:** a lying sensor can move R and W only earlier, or decline the bound, and either way hides nothing it could not hide already.
  - **W and R take each new value even when it is earlier than the one before,** since E can fall between two heartbeats, and R can then be after W by up to that fall. A move back only starts a later gap earlier, and a gap starts at the earlier of the two either way. **The one limit is the floor** (D6's B1 on #641 at `8f78d057`): no move back takes W or R below the end of a gap closed unwatched. A move below it would start a later gap inside that gap, which a covered close would then claim as watched, and a sensor that states early `at_utc`s could do it at will.
  - *Tests:* gap leg 16's fifth case, whose first control places W and R at their receipts and whose second bounds R and places W at its receipt (D6's N1 on #641 at `8f78d057`); and leg 17's ninth case, whose second half fails one that bounds W and places R at its receipt.
  - **With the sensor database lost, that moment is lost with it** (SL-31; `records-auditor` on #641). The gap at the node's start then reaches back to an earlier moment the node still holds durably, and never to a later one. That is a watched moment kept in the run file, if D5 keeps one there: a stale copy only moves the start earlier, which fails closed. **The copy is of the earlier of W and R** (D6's B2 at `467c1dd6`): a copy of W alone, with the cursor lost, would leave a flat stretch before W in no gap. With none, it is the time of the live enrolment's `sensor.enrolled` record. The gap's event then says that its start is a bound, not the moment watching stopped. Which of the two the node keeps is D5's; #644 keeps both, since `54d0191c`. Gap leg 9 tests it.
  - **A sensor never watched since its enrolment has been unwatched since it** (`records-auditor` on #641). Its gap, whether opened at a node start or while the node runs, begins no later than the time of the live enrolment's `sensor.enrolled` record. The reach-back is therefore not for a lost database only: it applies whenever the node holds no watched moment for the sensor.
    - **That gap stands from the enrolment, not from a connection's end** (D5's question on #644, comment 6839551239). A `link` gap otherwise opens when a connection ends or a dial fails, so a sensor whose first connection succeeds would leave the interval from its enrolment to its first current heartbeat in no gap. So while the node holds no watched moment for a sensor, it holds a gap for it from the time of the live enrolment's `sensor.enrolled` record, with cause `link` (`node-start` when it is found at a start, as above). It is written as any `link` gap is: `open` once it has stood 60 s and again when it closes, or once, `closed`, if it closes sooner (#644 §1.9 item 5). It closes at the first current heartbeat that shows capture, **unwatched** (`covered: false`): there is no watched moment for a chain to start from, and a backlog the sensor made before that heartbeat is its word, placed on no clock of the node's. A gap opened only at that heartbeat, as D5 first proposed, is not enough: through a long backlog, or a first dial that never comes, nothing would be `open` in the stream. *Test:* gap leg 15.
  - **Across a restart of the node, the reach-back compares wall-clock times,** the saved moment and the time after the start, because nothing else survives a restart (as for C8-5's cap). A clock stepped forward during the outage lengthens the gap, which fails closed. A clock stepped back can put the gap's end before its start: the gap is still recorded from the saved moment, never dropped, and names the step (`time.node-stepped`, which C4 has; D5 on #641). The step check at the `node-start` gap's close then caps W, R and the floor at the wall clock (the step rule, above; D6's B1 on #641 at `cebfff61`). `records-auditor` on #641 asked for this case. **Across a restart the chain's fourth part counts the gap's length from the saved W to the close, a wall-clock difference** (D3's sweep for D6's review at `02773aa1`). A step back during the stop would ask a chain for fewer heartbeats, by the step divided by `HeartbeatGapMax`, and one longer than the stop for none. **D1's ruling closes it where the step is found** (D1 to D3 on #641 at `bb7f0d91`): a step that the cap finds at the `node-start` gap's close marks that gap `clock_stepped`, and a marked gap closes unwatched, never as covered (the step rule, above). **What is left** (corrected for D6's N4 on #641 at `6723bf90`): a step back that leaves the saved W no later than the clock after the start, which the cap cannot see. Such a step can be as long as the time from the saved W to the start: the stop, plus W's age when the node stopped, which is up to a minute, since the cursor is saved at most a minute apart. It asks the chain for at most that time divided by 30 s fewer heartbeats, the stop divided by 30 s plus about two. Parts 1 to 3 still hold, and part 3 is judged by `at_utc`, so the shortfall matters only where the sensor's `at_utc` spans less of the stop than the node's clock did, that is, where the sensor's clock fell behind. Where the sensor's clock is independent of the node's, one that falls 2 s behind raises `sensor-clock-skew`. **Where one source serves both hosts, a step back it makes on both during the stop raises nothing,** and a stall of the sensor no longer than that step can then close as covered: bounded by the stop plus W's age, as above (D6's N3 on #641 at `c0cf1ecc`). A sensor that lies needs no residual (AP-36). **Only wall-clock times survive a reboot.** Across a restart of the node's process alone, the host's boot-relative clock survives too, and a check against it could see the step. **Not using it is D3's choice, on cost:** it needs that clock and the boot's identity saved with W, and a reboot would still leave the residual. Removing the residual altogether would mean writing every `node-start` gap as unwatched, so that every restart of the node would be recorded as unwatched.
  - **A step back seen while the node runs** (D6's N2 on #641 at `467c1dd6`). W, R and the run file's copy are wall-clock times, so after a step back of Δ, a value saved before it can be later than the last watched moment on the new clock, and a reach-back from it would start up to Δ late with nothing naming the step. So the node looks for a step under the step rule (above): before every time it takes from its wall clock for a gap, for W, R or the floor, or for the copy, and at its tick (D1's direction on D6's B1 on #641 at `02773aa1`). A step back of Δ found then **moves W, R, the floor, the copy and E's held samples back by Δ, and the node saves them at once,** the cursor and the run file both, marks the gap it affects `clock_stepped`, and writes `time.node-stepped` once for that step. A step forward needs nothing: it only lengthens a gap. *Test:* gap leg 9's fourth case, its fifth for the floor, its eighth for a step between two ticks, its ninth for E's samples and its tenth for the cap at a start, and leg 16's eighth for a step found at a close.
  - **Within a run, a gap's length is taken on the node's monotonic clock,** and its written times on the wall clock (D6's N1 on #641 at `9d74bb73`). A step forward while a gap is open therefore does not change the count that the chain's fourth part asks for. A step back is found by the step check and marks the gap, which then closes unwatched (D1's ruling on #641 at `bb7f0d91`; the step rule, above). A step back that puts the written end before the start is handled as across a restart: the gap is written, never dropped for its times, and names the step. *Test:* gap leg 9's third case.
  - **A gap is closed as covered only by a chain of heartbeats whose two ends the node placed itself.** The chain runs in one run, from the heartbeat that set the watched moment to a current heartbeat after the gap. The node placed each end itself, never later than its own receipt. Between them, the heartbeats the agent made while no connection was open arrive in a backlog, and the node cannot place them on its own clock. **A gap marked `clock_stepped` is never closed by a chain** (D1's ruling on #641 at `bb7f0d91`; the step rule, above). For any other gap, the chain shows capture only if all four of these hold (`records-auditor` and D5's N5 on #641):
      1. every `seq` of the run between the two ends is read, with no rise of D among them, that is, no declared loss (#644's wording);
      2. the chain shows capture: CH-31's blind rule by `at_utc`, flat counts included, as defined above, and not the carrier alone;
      3. **no two consecutive heartbeats are more than `HeartbeatGapMax` apart by their `at_utc`.** The two ends count, so a silence just before the reconnect fails too. Only `at_utc` enters this check: where the node places a live heartbeat on its own clock (W's rule, above) does not (D5's second point on #641 at `8f78d057`);
      4. **there are at least as many heartbeats as the gap's length on the node's clock divided by `HeartbeatGapMax`:** 20 for a ten-minute gap. **The length is counted from W, never from the gap's start at R** (D6's B1 on #641 at `da7a84ee`), since the chain begins at W's heartbeat. This ties the sensor's spacing to the node's clock, so that a sensor clock running slow cannot pass the third part with too few heartbeats. #644 has carried it since `54d0191c`, and D4 states it in CH-39 or CH-45.

      **Unbroken `seq`s alone do not show capture** (D5's N5 on #641): an engine that stops its heartbeats for eight minutes without a new run leaves them unbroken. Gap leg 7 tests the third part, and gap leg 8 the fourth.
    - **`HeartbeatGapMax` is 30 s,** three of C13's 10 s `SensorTickInterval`s (C13 0.2 §12, which defines it for the 0.1 heartbeat; D4 states the 0.2 heartbeat's cadence in CH-39), as #644 proposes. D4 states it in CH-39 as the one value that C13, the node's design and this note use. This note had 20 s at `d27efbe7`, which D3 gives up. At a 10 s tick, one missing heartbeat already spaces two by 20 s, so 20 s left nothing for a late one. Thirty seconds lets two consecutive heartbeats be missing, or one missing and the next up to 10 s late, so an honest stall on a loaded sensor does not record watched time as unwatched. The 10 s more that it lets pass as covered changes little against a compromised sensor, which can state any heartbeat it likes (AP-36).
    - **There is no partial cover** (#644 §1.9 item 5).
